(0.3%), Vue 0.353M (0.16%); across the policy census 684 of 1,135 second stages splice nothing.
Inside a recompile: the body walk 53%, finish_chunk 17%, the seeds 14%. The discards' fallback
template compiles, also main-thread work under the owner's count, are Djot's 1.56M cycles a run
(its three discards, all of spliced chunks). The exemplars: V8 and SpiderMonkey keep one bytecode
and one feedback store a function and put the optimized code on a per-function entry word (V8's
dispatch entry, js-function-inl.h:126-167; SpiderMonkey's jitCodeRaw, vm/JSScript.cpp:3487-3520),
so that one store redirects every caller and a deopt resumes in the baseline code that exists;
JSC copies a second CodeBlock that shares the baseline's bytecode and profiling metadata
(CopyParsedBlock, bytecode/CodeBlock.cpp:287-340) and relinks only the replaced code's callers.
The tier's code on the first stage (V8's structure) needs that per-chunk redirect, since A2 picks
up new code only on a new chunk (a global epoch move per install is P2's measured loser), and the
first stage's template code kept as the fallback.
So the order changes: point 13 first (compiling off the main thread, which takes generate's 12
ms off Djot's main thread and most of React's pass difference), then the per-chunk entry word
with the tier's code on the first stage and discards that fall back to code that exists (JSC's
jettison, V8's deopt), which together remove the empty plans' recompiles and the fallback
compiles (about 2.4% of Djot's pass); the spliced recompiles go with stage 3.

Step 12, compiling off the main thread (point 13; design, 2026-11-10; input
target/p3/stage2/offthread-input.txt). The exemplars split a compile in three. V8's Maglev
(63481c19): the job made on the main thread without reading feedback (Prepare; the broker and
persistent handles, maglev-compilation-info.cc:68-140); the graph, its feedback read through the
broker and memoized per site, register allocation, assembly and by default the Code object on a
background thread (Execute; maglev-compiler.cc:90-313; maglev_build_code_on_background,
flag-definitions.h:657), at most two (concurrent_maglev_max_threads, 694-696); dependencies
committed, weak objects registered and the code stored in the dispatch entry on the main thread
(Finalize; maglev-compiler.cc:316-384, compiler.cc:4706-4775), from the stack guard's
INSTALL_MAGLEV_CODE interrupt (maglev-concurrent-dispatcher.cc:315-320, 391-414); while a job is
pending the function is marked tiering and its budget is out of reach (tiering-manager.cc:213-216);
synchronous when the dispatcher is off (compiler.cc:1258-1293). SpiderMonkey: the WarpSnapshot on
the main thread, holding what "WarpBuilder can't read off-thread without racing" (jit/
WarpSnapshot.h:53-56; jit/Ion.cpp:1775-1792), the bytecode read off thread; WarpBuilder, the
optimizations, register allocation and code generation into a buffer on a helper thread (Ion.cpp:
1750-1771, 1906-1918); attached at an interrupt and linked at the next call, with the dependencies
checked and the code copied into executable memory on the main thread (vm/Runtime.cpp:403-422;
Ion.cpp:357-405); off thread only with more than one CPU (Ion.cpp:2110-2118). JSC: everything,
the profiles' reads (under each CodeBlock's lock) and the code's mapping included, in a compiler
thread (dfg/DFGPlan.cpp:186-; DFGSpeculativeJIT.cpp:175-194); validation, a cross-modifying-code
fence and the install on the main thread (DFGPlan.cpp:574-640; DFGJITFinalizer.cpp:56-88). The
tier: the builder reads the snapshot, immutable chunk data and one lazily filled cell; the back
end also reads live chunk state (the compact templates' preferred ways, the inline targets' weak
pins, lazily built activation facts and resolution sites, the agent heap's allocation sites, R)
and may assemble a process-wide stub (offthread-input.txt 2.2-2.3); the graph, the build's output,
the allocation, the assembler and the exit tables are Send, the chunk, the snapshot (one raw
pointer the builder never reads), the builder and code generator (they hold &Chunk) and the code
objects are not (2.5); the code is position-independent, emitted into words and copied into
executable memory at the end (2.6). Of generate's time on Djot after step 10: the build 44.7%,
register allocation 18.6%, emission 18.4%, executable memory 10.0% (before step 9), the snapshot
2.6%, the install 2.2% (3.2).
The step:
1. Where each part runs: the snapshot on the main thread (SpiderMonkey's oracle; point 3's
   immutable summary); the build, register allocation, emission and the copy into executable
   memory on a compile thread (V8's Execute with its Code object, JSC's compiler thread); the
   install on the main thread. Departure from V8 and JSC, measured: the feedback is read on the
   main thread. The caches and records are Cells that helpers and generated code write, so a
   concurrent read would race unless every feedback cell were atomic or locked (V8 keeps the main
   thread the sole writer and locks two-slot groups, feedback-vector.h:398-406), and the snapshot is
   2.6% of generate (0.43% of Djot's pass).
2. The job's inputs, all Send: the snapshot, extended with every live read of the back end (each
   named site's preferred way, the inline targets' callee words and environments with their pins
   upgraded on the main thread, the activation facts and resolution sites forced there, the
   allocation sites' cursor indices and cell sizes, R and the exit limit, the layouts; a call
   target's function as an address word); a frozen view of the chunk's immutable data (ops, names'
   text, constant words, frame, flags, positions, slot names, the inline targets' data, the name
   statics and globals, the chunk's cell addresses and images), which the builder and the back end
   read through a trait the live chunk also implements, so that the emitters the template JIT
   shares stay one code; and the code generator's options and switches, read on the main thread.
   The job reads no heap memory, so the collector needs neither to trace nor to cancel it
   (SpiderMonkey traces its snapshots and JSC suspends its threads because theirs do).
3. One compile thread a process, started at the first job, serving every engine (agents and
   workers run engines on other threads); a job carries its engine's result queue and poll word.
   One thread because the measured queue is short (offthread-input.txt 3.4: a Djot job ends 0.32
   ms after its checkpoint on average, 1.70 ms at most) and the owner's model has two CPUs, one
   of them the main thread's. The process-wide stubs the tier calls are assembled on the main
   thread before the first job.
4. While a job is pending its chunk has no checkpoint armed and its profile says compiling (V8's
   tiering_in_progress, SpiderMonkey's isIonCompilingOffThread); the function runs the code it
   has, the first stage's or a discarded code's fallback, until the install.
5. Finishing: the compile thread queues the result and sets a new bit of the engine's poll word
   (already an atomic shared by Arc and set from other threads, interrupt.rs:36-90); the next poll
   at an entry, a back edge or after an allocating helper installs every finished job (V8's
   interrupt, SpiderMonkey's attach). The install checks what no run-time guard covers: the chunk
   is still the one to install into (not superseded, not published by a chain recompile, still
   warming up for a recompile), its function's runtime lives, the realm whose global environment
   the snapshot pinned is the running one, no discard flagged sites since the snapshot; otherwise
   it drops the result and the function may tier up again (V8 drops a job whose dependencies fail
   "s.t. we may reattempt compilation", maglev-compiler.cc:359-368). Then the JitCode, the exit
   table, the slot and the publication as today, after an instruction barrier on the main thread
   (JSC's crossModifyingCodeFence; the writer's flush synchronizes only the writer). A refusal ends
   as one ends today.
6. Executable memory: the compile thread copies the code into the region under its Mutex and
   flushes it. The capacity's reclaim is the main thread's (CLOCK, a thread-local registry), so a
   job denied capacity returns its words and the install maps them after the main thread's
   reclaim (SpiderMonkey's link, as the fallback).
7. The global environment's pin is taken with the snapshot, not at the install; the engine's list
   of pending jobs keeps each job's chunk and first stage alive.
8. Synchronous, today's path: with one CPU available to the process (std::thread::
   available_parallelism, which honors the affinity mask where SpiderMonkey counts configured
   CPUs: a process confined to one CPU gains nothing from the thread); where no thread can be
   spawned (wasm32, a failed spawn, which degrades as the watchdog's does); under
   TRUST_JS_OPT_SYNC=1; and in the tests that read a compile's results at once (V8's
   --single-threaded, SpiderMonkey's runtime switch, JSC's useConcurrentJIT=false). The gates run
   the tier's configurations both ways.
9. An engine dropped with jobs pending marks them cancelled; the compile thread skips them and
   their results are dropped.
10. The log: each job's time on the compile thread apart from the main-thread parts (step 11's
   lines, the snapshot's and the install's), so the sessions count the main thread's share as the
   owner's bar does.
- In two parts, each reviewable: (a) generate split into the snapshot and frozen view on the
  main thread, a compile function of Send inputs and Send outputs, and the install, run
  synchronously; masked code hashes identical to main on the fixtures, Richards, Crypto and the
  tier's tests; (b) the thread, the queue, the poll bit, the checks at the install, the
  synchronous mode, teardown.
- Tests: (a)'s hashes; a function gets the tier's code after its job ends, at the next poll; a job
  whose chunk was superseded, discarded or whose function was collected meanwhile is dropped and the
  function may tier up again; an engine dropped with jobs pending; one CPU (an affinity mask of
  one) compiles synchronously; the library's suite, Test262's subsets and the tier's tests with
  the tier on in both modes; x86-64, Windows and wasm32 build (the thread is the tier's target's
  only).
- Expected (offthread-input.txt 3.3, scaled to session p3s2-7f92ab9d): generate's main-thread part
  0.79% of Djot's pass, 0.32% of React's, 0.20% of Vue's, against 16.22%, 5.50% and 2.58% today;
  with the recompiles and fallback compiles still on the main thread until the next step 5.49%,
  1.64% and 0.85%. Unmeasured: a job's hand-off and the later install's cost in template code.

Step 11 landed (bbc8e8e2..838c1865, 2026-11-10; target/p3/stage2/step11-impl). As designed, with
the tier on; the tier off keeps P2's schedule and counts, its direct gate byte-identical.
(1) opt::ENTRY_WEIGHT (K = 64): the direct gate adds K and compares in 64 bits (a count within K
of u32::MAX takes the helper, which saturates), the construct take-back subtracts it, the shared
stubs' table keys it; the Rust entry paths (H_CALL_HIT, note_native_entry, run_borrowed_frame)
read Interp::opt_entry_weight, the engine's copy made with it, as opt_exit_every is (the first
build read a OnceLock there, which gave note_native_entry a stack frame and the tier off +0.25%
instructions in TypeScript). compile_entry records each header's span in its backward-jump scan
and gives the Asm each header's weight (Asm::loop_weights); a count that would reach the word
stops one below it (eight instructions while pending, eleven once due, step 8's seven); a rotated
loop's countdown adds its iterations times the weight (MOVZ, MADD). (2) next_inline_checkpoint:
(100, 400, 1,600, 6,400) x K, then 500 x K; warm_up_gap and warm_up_word likewise << R; nothing
armed past the word (a discard whose warm-up would pass it logs "not reoptimized ...: its
warm-up would pass the checkpoint word"); `last` and the compile on unsettled feedback gone; R's
cap 13. (3) The fingerprint as designed. (4) MAX_OPTIMIZED_OPS = 15,000: generate refuses before
the snapshot, and try_inline_recompile stops a first stage over it whose plan is empty before its
bytecode recompile (refuses_unspliced, disarmed as the empty plan's refusal is), so zlib's a1
(21,869 operations) now costs nothing where main spent 3.14 ms of generate and a recompile on it.
(5) The main-thread parts of a tier compile logged (838c1865): `[opt] main-thread
recompile|install|fallback <name> in <ms> ms`, the deferred fallback's compile included; no clock
read without the log.
- Measured (fat LTO, the branch against main 0bb3041f; the fixtures on CPU 10, medians of 5;
  Octane's fixed runs on CPUs 10-13, medians of 3): Octane -0.88% (aBc -0.88%): TypeScript -2.19%,
  DeltaBlue -6.83%, Crypto -1.14%, RegExp -1.09%, every benchmark within 0.3 points of aBc; the
  tier off within 0.05% but TypeScript's +0.18%, the incremental collector's time-bounded slices
  (with TRUST_JS_GC_INCREMENTAL=0 equal within 0.003%). The fixtures compile exactly aBc's
  functions; instructions: Vue +0.01% and +0.02% (the measured pass, passes 2-10), React +0.04%
  and +0.01%, Djot +1.09% and -0.53% (aBc +1.1%, -0.4%); generate Djot +9.2% (aBc +8.0%). Cycles
  at equal work differ by placement: Vue +1.40% and +3.24% (front-end stalls, I-cache refills,
  mispredictions; the tier-off Vue pass +1.66% at +0.01% instructions with identical template code,
  and round 1's binary, with the same code, +0.88% for passes 2-10), React +0.21% and +0.31%, Djot
  +1.39% and -0.24%: the X925 session is the bar's reading. No compile on unsettled feedback in the
  fixtures or Octane (main: TypeScript 96, GBEMU 6, Box2D 5, PdfJS 3, DeltaBlue 1, Earley-Boyer 1).
  Richards' schedule in run 4; Crypto's 40 runs 33 compiles and 7 discards.
- The main-thread parts (CPU 10, medians a run): Djot's recompiles 2.88 ms (38), installs 0.024
  ms, fallbacks 2.09 ms (3), beside generate's 19.31 ms; React 0.36, 0.005 and 0.34 ms beside 2.73;
  Vue 1.71, 0.009 and 0.25 ms beside 7.28. Octane's 5-run logs: recompiles 104 ms, installs 0.85
  ms, fallbacks 219 ms (TypeScript's 133 ms in 124) against 843 ms of generate.
- Tests: the design's list (the 500th entry; a long function's short loop; nested loops; a rotated
  loop's countdown and bails; the quiet window after the fourth checkpoint and feedback that never
  settles; the fingerprint's refills and transitions; both size-limit paths; saturation below the
  word and nothing past it; R's cap; the tier off; a direct entry at the top of the word), and 11
  mutation checks, each removing one piece of the step, all caught (the direct gate's first test
  missed its 32-bit compare and was rewritten). The library's suites with the tier off and on
  eager (3,141 each, 10 ignored), the tier's tests eager with exits every 7 and with an exit limit
  of 1 (244 each), the poll and Agent tests with the tier on (51); clippy as main, fmt clean. On
  zombie (x86-64): the check with tests, clippy with -D warnings and Windows' check clean (date.rs's
  20 warnings), the library's suite passes (2,773).
- Departures: the engine's copy of the weight (a test that switches the tier after making an
  engine keeps the old weight in the Rust entry paths); the fingerprint test retries its evictions
  until they fall in one epoch, since other test threads move the process's call-cache epoch.
- Open: Vue's cycles at equal work (placement); the deferred fallback's log line, which no
  workload reached, checked by reading.

zlib's a8 (record, 2026-11-10; target/p3/stage2/step11-impl/marked/br11-on.oct-zlib.err and the
last coverage run's coverage-p3s2-7f92ab9d/octane/err.log). The 8.3 points of Octane's coverage
that a8 (2,462 operations) spends in template code labeled "(second stage)" are no mislabel: its
tier code exits at pc 278 (NotNumber) early in each call, the exited frame goes on in the VM,
whose loop moves into the chunk's borrowed-frame template code (tiering.rs OsrCodeState; its
compile is logged right after the first exit), and the long call runs there to its end. The code
is discarded only at its 100th exit, a hundred calls later, and then recompiled with the site
declined (R 1), after which it runs without exits. Three other zlib functions do the same
(fn(d,f,g,h), 50 exits at each of two sites; fn(b,d,f,g); fn(f,g,h,i)). The exemplars discard
sooner. JSC keeps 100 << R for exits (osrExitCountForReoptimization, runtime/OptionsList.h:397)
but 5 << R when the exited frame loops (osrExitCountForReoptimizationFromLoop, :398): the baseline
loop's tier-up check in an exited frame (one whose code block has a replacement, so it came from
an exit) jettisons the replacement once its exits reach that threshold (operationOptimize,
jit/JITOperations.cpp:2944-2966; CodeBlock::shouldReoptimizeFromLoopNow, bytecode/CodeBlock.cpp:
2758-2771, shifted by R at 2740-2751), and an exit inside an inlined function that has tried to
enter a loop uses it at once (dfg/DFGOSRExitCompilerCommon.cpp:70-104; setDidTryToEnterInLoop,
dfg/DFGOSREntry.cpp:226). SpiderMonkey invalidates after 10 fixable bailouts, whatever follows
them (frequentBailoutThreshold, jit/JitOptions.cpp:262; IonScript::shouldInvalidate,
jit/IonScript.h:336-338; jit/BaselineBailouts.cpp:2204-2224); V8 at its first eager
deoptimization. Step 3 left JSC's loop limit to stage 5 because "the exited frame finishes in the
VM, whose loops count nothing" (driver.rs's module doc), but the VM's loop transfer into
borrowed-frame code is where JSC's check sits.

Step 13, the exit limit of an exited frame that loops (proposed; after step 12 lands, since step
12 reworks the exit table's construction and the discard path). JSC's rule for the outermost
frame, the only frame the tier has until stage 3: when the VM moves a frame into its chunk's
borrowed-frame code (the loop transfer) while the chunk's entry code is the tier's, so that the
frame came from an exit, and that code's exits have reached 5 << R (ExitTable::loop_limit, made
beside `limit`), the code is discarded as at its limit (opt::discard: flags, R + 1, the template
fallback, the warm-up), and the frame goes on into its borrowed-frame code as now. The loop must
be hot, as JSC's is (revised 2026-11-10, before any code): every exit that does not jettison
resets the baseline's execution counter to a long warm-up (thresholdForOptimizeAfterLongWarmUp,
1,000 scaled by the code's size and 2^R; a loop iteration counts 1, an entry 15:
dfg/DFGOSRExitCompilerCommon.cpp:115-141, runtime/OptionsList.h:373-377, bytecode/CodeBlock.cpp:
2602-2608), so the from-loop check in operationOptimize runs only once the exited frame's own loop
has warmed up again, not for a cold loop. TRust's exited frame transfers at its first back edge
(run_native_frame_in_vm's VmTiering::hot, which leaves the VM, the slowest place to loop, at once),
so that transfer alone would fire on cold loops as on hot ones (the departure that deferred this
rule in step 3). So: an exit that brings the code's count to its loop limit or past it, the code
not discarded, runs its frame under the VM's own warm-up (VmTiering::new: 128 back edges and 4,096
units of loop work) in place of hot; when that frame's loop asks for the transfer, the code is
discarded and the frame moves into its borrowed-frame code as now; a frame whose loop ends first
finishes in the VM. Below the loop limit an exited frame transfers at its first back edge as now.
The log names the loop limit. Tests: a function whose tier code exits early in each call and then
loops long is discarded at its fifth such call, not its hundredth (its tenth at R = 1); one whose
loop after the exit is short is not, and its exits count to 100 << R as now; exits that no loop
transfer follows count to 100 << R as now; the tier off unchanged. Expected: zlib's four functions
discard after 5 calls in place of 100, most of a8's 8.3 points of Octane's coverage move to tier
code, and zlib's fixed runs execute fewer instructions (to be measured as step 11's were).

The owner's answer on the fallback compiles (2026-11-10): "You can leave the fallback compiles out
if you think it's best." They are left out of the 3% count, which counts the bytecode recompile
that makes the tier's chunk, the snapshot, generate while it runs on the main thread, and the
install; the fallback compiles stay logged (step 11's `main-thread fallback` lines) and every
session reports them beside the bar. Why: a fallback compile makes the template JIT's code for a
second stage, which the tier-off engine compiles for every spliced second stage at its first
native entry, so with the tier on these compiles are a subset of the tier-off engine's, not new
work (Djot's three are all of spliced chunks); none of the exemplars compiles at a
deoptimization, since their baseline code exists, and their compile-time counts are the
optimizing compile's own; and the time stays in the measured passes, which the bar of no fixture
more than 2% slower with the tier on counts whole. Effect on the expectations (step 11's logs
scaled to session p3s2-7f92ab9d's Djot pass): after step 12 about 3.4% of Djot's pass counted
(5.3% with the fallback compiles), after step 14 about 2.6% (4.5% with them: step 14 removes the
empty plans' recompiles and fallbacks, and Djot's fallbacks are of spliced chunks); the two-CPU
session measures them.

Splicing's worth with the tier on, and the inlining input (2026-11-10; target/p3/stage2/
splice-worth, target/p3/stage2/inlining-input.txt). Main's code (step 11's fat-LTO binary), the
tier on with its splicing against TRUST_JS_INLINE_BUDGET=0 TRUST_JS_INLINE_SMALL_BUDGET=0 (every
plan empty), one CPU (CPU 4: instructions are the reading, its cycles shared with builds):
- Octane's fixed runs without splicing: +4.12% instructions (geomean; DeltaBlue +19.3%, Splay
  +17.1%, Mandreel +14.8%, Crypto +8.9%, TypeScript +8.5%, Earley-Boyer +2.6%, GBEMU +1.3%;
  Richards -6.6%, RayTrace -0.8%; NavierStokes, zlib, RegExp and CodeLoad splice nothing), -5.67%
  against the tier off where splicing gives -9.41%.
- The fixtures without splicing: Vue's measured pass +12.75% instructions and its passes 2-10
  +4.72% (worse than the tier off's +8.58% and +2.27%); React's measured pass -4.00%, its passes
  2-10 equal (its one discard gone: a self-hosted builtin's callback site, whose splice fails for
  every caller but the first); Djot's -3.89% and -1.71% (discards 3 to 1, exits 402 to 202, all
  200 WrongCallee exits gone; generate 22.6 to 18.6 ms, 47 compiles against 39).
- So splicing stays until the tier inlines in its graph: it is worth 4% of Octane and 13% of
  Vue's pass, and what it costs Djot and React is its identity guards' failures. WrongCallee, the
  splice guard's exit, is 22,937 of the policy census's 52,446 exits and names a site in 115 of
  its 326 discards; Djot skips 16 ways a run whose closure died (sites that meet a new closure of
  one literal per call, inferred). The exemplars inline no call site with several callees
  (Maglev only a polymorphic load's constant-function arms, maglev-graph-builder.cc:6038-6222;
  Warp's trial inlining skips a site with more than one stub, TrialInlining.cpp:865-878) and guard
  varying closures by their code (SpiderMonkey's later stubs by script, GuardFunctionScript,
  CacheIR.cpp:6607-6623; V8 by feedback cell; JSC by executable); SpiderMonkey gives a
  self-hosted builtin's callback site a private feedback copy per caller (createInlinedICScript,
  TrialInlining.cpp:809-861).
- The rest of the inlining input, for stage 3's design: the tier's pipeline is one chunk
  throughout (pcs, frame states, the verifier, the exits, the snapshot); every operation the tier
  does not lower runs its template on ctx.chunk's canonical frame, so an inlined callee's effects,
  calls among them, have no frame of their own: callees without effects first (E0), then a
  canonical frame per inlined callee switched around its effects (E1, JSC's InlineCallFrame
  layout) or templates taking their chunk and frame (E2); a multi-frame exit can be composed from
  today's VM pieces (the inner frame run to its end, then the outer frame from its call's pc + 1
  with the result pushed, or at the call with the throw pending); no per-site call counts on main
  (the parked e854636e counts per way), and Maglev's frequency rule needs a caller's invocation
  count, which jit_runs no longer is. Two corrections: point 8's Maglev numbers (callees under 30
  bytes inline eagerly to depth 10 within a 3,000-byte total; 27 bytes, or 75 with float inputs or
  outputs, is the size exempt from the 0.95 frequency rule; the depth limit is the inlining
  function's, so other callees reach depth 2), and point 7's exemplar (JSC's unlinked DFG, whose
  invalidation points the plan adopts, inlines nothing, DFGByteCodeParser.cpp:2075-2078; its
  linked DFG and FTL do).
- Steps 12 to 14 do not change. If the measured passes still miss the 2% bar after them, the
  splicing policy is the next lever: only call sites with one callee, or guards by code, as the
  exemplars inline.

Step 14, the tier's code on the first stage (design, 2026-11-10; input target/p3/stage2/
recompile-input.txt sections 1, 3.B, 3.C, 4 and 5.1; after step 13). For an empty plan the tier
compiles the function's first-stage chunk itself, as V8 and SpiderMonkey compile a function's one
bytecode, and no second chunk is made; spliced plans keep their second stages until stage 3.
1. try_inline_recompile: an empty plan with the tier on makes no compile_with_inlines and
   publishes nothing: step 12's job compiles the first stage (its snapshot of the first stage's own
   caches and records, no record map) and installs into it at the next poll; the first stage's
   checkpoints end as now. The done test covers a first stage that carries the tier's code or has
   its job pending, or a later checkpoint (the chain request's re-arm, supersede's) would build a
   second stage on top. The size limit (refuses_unspliced) and a refusal keep the first stage's
   template code as now, with no recompile to drop.
2. Where the code goes (recompile-input's B2; SpiderMonkey's JitScript holds the Baseline and Ion
   code side by side, jit/JitScript.h:295-400): the tier's code into the first stage's code slot
   (chunk.jit, replace_resident, as reoptimize's install does), the template code it replaces
   kept on the chunk as the fallback instead of retired. CLOCK may evict either, as now; a discard
   whose kept code is gone compiles one, as today's discard does.
3. The redirect (B's R2): the chunk's entry word, a u32 beside its runs and retry words that
   counts its slot's code changes; a call way copies it at its fill (CallIc's free bytes 60-63,
   after epoch) and every path that enters a way's code compares it: the direct sequence (it loads
   the callee chunk and its retry word already), jit_call_hit, resolve_cached_call,
   prepared_call_current, the construct sites and the overflow table. A mismatch takes the slow
   path, which refills from the chunk's current code. The exemplars load the current entry at each
   call (SpiderMonkey's jitCodeRaw, vm/JSScript.cpp:3487-3520; V8's dispatch entry,
   objects/js-function-inl.h:126-167); a TRust way caches more than the entry (the code's memory,
   its pc table, the CLOCK reference), all of it the code's, so it checks one word and refills; a
   global epoch move per install (R1) is P2's measured loser (v8-chase-p2-plan-2026-10-26.txt:
   6558-6602). Template code compiled with the tier off emits no compare and stays byte-identical
   (as step 11's direct gate), since without the tier no slot's code changes in place.
   Correctness stays on today's mechanism: after an install a stale way enters the kept template
   code, which is valid; a discard and a reoptimization move the global epoch as now, besides the
   entry word, so no stale way can enter discarded code once it is released. A missed compare
   costs speed, not correctness; leaving the epoch at discards to the entry word alone belongs to
   the dependencies' invalidation points (point 7), once every path is proven.
4. The discard falls back to code that exists (JSC's jettison installs the baseline code,
   bytecode/CodeBlock.cpp:2400-2434; V8's deopt points the dispatch entry at the interpreter,
   objects/code.cc:166-224): the kept template code goes back into the slot, and swap_in_template
   compiles only where the kept code is gone. R + 1, the flags (on the first stage), the exit and
   loop limits and the warm-up as now; the warm-up's gate admits a first stage that carried the
   tier's code, on its inline_retry_at, free since its checkpoints ended. Reoptimize: generate on
   the first stage as now; its install keeps the template code as the fallback again.
5. The chain recompile (reoptimize_without_property_chains) is unchanged: it supersedes the first
   stage, the tier's code included, as it supersedes a spliced second stage and its tier code
   today; a job of a chunk superseded meanwhile is dropped at the install (step 12's check).
6. Exits resume on the first stage's ops and its borrowed-frame code (ctx.chunk is the first
   stage), step 13's loop limit unchanged. The exit profile's keys that were chunk2's unmapped pcs
   are the first stage's, which a caller's splice of this function then reads under the
   inline-kind rules, as JSC keeps one exit profile per function that its inlined uses read too
   (bytecode/UnlinkedCodeBlock.h:322-334; each inlined frame's parser entry takes its callee's,
   dfg/DFGByteCodeParser.cpp:11696).
7. The census and coverage: no "[tier] inlined 0 site(s)" line for these, "[opt] compiled" names
   the first stage as now, and the kept code is labeled the first stage's (template_class). The
   coverage tool (coverage2.py, which joins samples to code by time) counts a first stage's
   template samples taken after the tier's first install into it as fallback, or the bar's
   denominator would lose them; the sessions sum the main-thread lines as now, without recompile
   lines for these.
- Tests: an empty plan's function runs the tier's code on its first stage with no second chunk; a
  way filled before the install enters the tier's code after it, with no epoch move, through each
  path (the direct sequence, jit_call_hit, resolve_cached_call, prepared_call_current, a construct
  site, the overflow table); a discard restores the kept code without a compile, and compiles one
  when the kept code was evicted; the warm-up and reoptimization on the first stage; the done test;
  the chain recompile superseding a first stage that carries the tier's code; a job whose first
  stage was superseded meanwhile dropped; the exit profile on the first stage read by a caller's
  splice; the tier off byte-identical.
- Expected: the empty plans' recompiles gone (684 of the census's 1,135 second stages; per run
  Djot's 0.866M cycles, 0.69 ms, 0.9% of its pass; React's 0.119M, 0.3%; Vue's 0.353M, 0.16%),
  with their chunks' memory and the fallback compiles of empty-plan discards (none on the
  fixtures, 104 in the census); Djot's counted main-thread share about 2.6% of its pass (the owner's
  answer's record). Cost: a load and a compare per direct call and per Rust probe with the tier on
  (Octane's fixed runs and the call microbenchmarks measure it). Risks: the fallback is the first
  stage's template code as compiled at its first native entry (no acyclic regions; compact
  templates baked from the caches then), where today's is compiled at the discard from mature
  caches, so the runs between a discard and its reoptimization may be slower (measured: the
  fallback samples' share on the fixtures and Octane); and the compare on every call path.

The fixtures' timer: performance.now() in the engine shell (design, 2026-11-10; the owner's
answer: "performance.now() would be great to add, that's approved."). The fixtures time their
passes with Date.now(), whole milliseconds: React's measured pass (about 30 ms) moves in 3.3% steps
against the 2% bar, Djot's (about 75 ms) in 1.3% steps, and the steady state's passes (fx2, one
pass a number) likewise. The engine shell has no performance object; the runtime CLI has
trust-js-web's (op_perf_now: the monotonic clock since its first use, not coarsened), and Node's is
what the fixtures read on Node's side. The exemplars' shells all define one: d8 a plain object
(now, mark, measure, measureMemory; src/d8/d8.cc:4872, 4983-4997) whose now is the monotonic clock
since the shell's start in milliseconds with a fraction, not coarsened (Shell::GetTimestamp,
d8.cc:2640-2648, 2697-2701; kInitialTicks at 632), deterministic under --predictable; JSC a
non-enumerable plain object (now, mark, measure; jsc.cpp:930-934) whose now is the monotonic clock
since its first call, reduced to 20 us (jsc.cpp:3494-3498; WTF Seconds::highTimePrecision),
beside preciseTime (wall-clock seconds; jsc.cpp:791, 2470-2473); SpiderMonkey an object (now,
mozMemory; js/src/shell/js.cpp:10993-10998, 12204-12226) whose now aliases dateNow, the wall clock
in milliseconds with microseconds (js.cpp:3589-3594). The change takes d8's and JSC's monotonic
clock, not coarsened (as d8's, the runtime's and Node's):
1. The engine: Engine::unstable_install_shell_performance() (doc-hidden like the other unstable
   methods, outside the embed gate, which the shell is built without) defines
   globalThis.performance, a plain object with Object.prototype, non-enumerable as built-ins are
   (JSC's DontEnum), with now() (length 0): the milliseconds since a process-wide origin (a
   OnceLock<Instant> set by the first install, the shell's start, as d8's kInitialTicks) as an f64
   at the clock's own resolution (std's Instant: CLOCK_MONOTONIC on Linux). No realm gets it
   otherwise: embedders define their own performance (the runtime's web glue, the browser's), and
   $262.createRealm's realms get none (d8's do; nothing here needs it).
2. The shell (bin/trust-js.rs) calls it after Engine::new(), for files, modules, stdin and the
   REPL; --help lists it under Globals beside print.
3. The fixtures (benchmarks/fixtures/vue.js, react.js, djot.js), the timer lines only, as the call
   microbenchmarks already time (target/claude-closeout/bench/micro/calls.js:4, 9): at the first
   timer line, var now = typeof performance !== "undefined" ? () => performance.now() :
   () => Date.now(); the start from now(), and elapsed_ms = Math.round((now() - start) * 100) /
   100. Every line before the first timer line stays byte-identical, so the warm copies
   (target/p3/stage2/step11-impl/fx/*-warm.js, cut at that line) stay valid. PROVENANCE.txt
   records the change with the old and new hashes and the re-baseline: elapsed numbers before it
   compare only within their own sessions; the measured pass's instructions change by the timer's
   calls (a few hundred).
4. The steady-state copies: new ones under target/p3/stage2/fx3 (the new fixture with fx2's nine
   more passes on the same now(), rounded alike); fx2 stays for the records and the runs that use
   it. The two-CPU session and later briefs use fx3.
5. What else changes on the shell, said so that no one reads it as the engine's loss or gain:
   Octane's base.js falls back to Date.now when the global has no performance.now (base.js:29-38),
   and Splay's and Mandreel's latency scores time their pauses with it (splay.js:98-148,
   mandreel.js:76-89, 18055): on our shell they move from Date's milliseconds to the monotonic
   clock, as Node's and d8's are already, so SplayLatency, MandreelLatency and Octane's Score (whose
   geomean includes them) are not comparable with records before the change, while comparisons
   within a session (the tier on and off) stay like for like and the comparison with Node becomes
   so. The call microbenchmarks (calls.js:4) switch alike on our shell. Web Tooling is unchanged:
   Benchmark.js times with Date unless the global object has process (dist/cli.js:179205,
   180829); its bundled process shim reads performance.now only for a tool's own timing labels.
   ARES-6 runs on the runtime CLI, which has performance.now already; vueshift stays there.
- Tests (crates/trust-js/tests/shell_performance.rs, driving the shell's binary as math_random.rs
  does): performance an object, non-enumerable, now a function of length 0; its values numbers,
  never decreasing across 10,000 calls, with a step below a millisecond among them; the first
  reading under a minute (the origin is the shell's start); a hot loop calling it under
  --tier=jit; the fixtures' outputs as PROVENANCE's expected fields, on the shell and on Node.
- Landing: after the two running agents' measurements (they read benchmarks/fixtures and
  attrib/fx2 from main's tree) and after step 12, whose rebase this does not touch. The two-CPU
  session runs with it.

Step 15, the exemplars' splice rule (design, 2026-11-10; input target/p3/stage2/
splice-policy-input.txt, the probe 911f9b8e on p3-splice-probe, never landed). With the tier on,
plan_inlines_at splices no call site whose ways hold more than one distinct callee, and none that
has met another closure of a way's function:
1. Several callees: Maglev inlines no polymorphic call (only a polymorphic load's constant-
   function arms; inlining-input.txt 1.1.6), and Warp's trial inlining skips a site with more than
   one stub (jit/TrialInlining.cpp:865-878). JSC's DFG inlines polymorphic calls, so this half is
   V8's and SpiderMonkey's rule.
2. Another closure: all three keep inlining such a site, guarded by the callee's code instead of
   its identity. SpiderMonkey's later stubs guard the script (GuardFunctionScript, jit/CacheIR.cpp:
   6607-6623). JSC despecifies a site that has seen closures to its executable (bytecode/
   CallLinkStatus.cpp:228-232; dfg/DFGByteCodeParser.cpp:1932-1954). V8's call feedback moves to
   the closures' shared feedback cell (src/builtins/ic-callable.tq:132-148). A splice pins its
   callee's identity and its environment, so until stage 3 builds callees into the caller's graph
   with a code guard and the environment read from the callee, not splicing is stage 2's form of
   their rule.
3. The record of a code-keyed hit. V8 writes the feedback cell once, at the transition
   (ic-callable.tq:148), and its steady state compares without writing (107, 137). The probe's
   sticky byte cost two instructions at every code-keyed hit (+0.06M to +0.81M instructions in the
   measured passes, the tier off's too). The step records a site's first code-keyed hit once: the
   site's code hint starts at a value the hinted-way test cannot hit, so the first code-keyed hit
   takes the full code probe, which writes the hint as it does today, and the planner reads that
   the hint was written. Where the hint's encoding cannot carry that safely at no cost, the
   fallback is one instruction on the hit paths (a store of the zero register into a byte that
   starts non-zero). The Rust paths record too: resolve_cached_call_entry's code-sharing retry
   (the VM's and the prepared calls') and jit_call_hit's live-closure path.
4. The tier off keeps P2's policy. It is the bar's baseline, the template JIT's spliced guard
   failures are not the tier's exits and discards, and splicing's measured worth with the tier off
   stands. The hint's initial value is the only change it sees: one full probe at a site's first
   code-keyed hit.
5. A site that meets its second closure after its splice: its guard's WrongCallee exits run to the
   exit limit as now, and the reoptimization's plan reads the record. Check that the
   reoptimization plans again rather than reusing the old splice; where it does not, the discard
   flags the site, as stage 2's discards flag sites.
6. Skipped sites leave empty plans where a function had nothing else to splice, which step 14
   compiles on the first stage.
- Expected, from the probe's instructions against main's policy:
  - Octane's geomean -1.8%: Richards -12.5%, DeltaBlue -7.7%, RayTrace -3.9%; Splay, Mandreel,
    Crypto and TypeScript kept.
  - React's measured pass -3.4%, which puts it 3.1% under the tier off.
  - Djot's measured pass -0.9% and its passes 2-10 -1.7%.
  - Vue unchanged.
  - Every WrongCallee exit on the fixtures gone.
  Djot's measured pass stays +4.6% over the tier off in instructions. That is compile work for
  splices that buy nothing on its first ten parses; step 12 takes it off the main thread, but
  not out of the instruction count.
- Tests:
  - a two-callee site not spliced with the tier on, and spliced with it off;
  - a site of fresh closures of one function (React's map callback) not spliced once a code-keyed
    hit happened, through each path that records it;
  - a one-closure site spliced as now;
  - the record's cost: nothing on an identity hit, and the template code's instructions on a
    code-keyed hit;
  - a late second closure's next plan;
  - the tier off's plans unchanged.
- Order: after step 13 lands, beside step 14. Its call paths are near step 14's entry word, so the
  agent that lands second rebases.

Step 12 landed (b8b38dd5..5f333a74, 2026-11-10; target/p3/stage2/step12-impl). As designed:
(a) a tier compile is the main thread's preparation (driver::prepare: the snapshot, Chunk::freeze's
frozen view opt::view::FrozenChunk, a64::Switches, the layouts, the realm's pin), a compile of Send
inputs and outputs (opt::job::compile: the build, verification, allocation, emission into words
and, off the main thread, the copy into executable memory without the main thread's reclaim) and
the main thread's link and landing; the emitters the template JIT shares are generic over
jit::JitChunk (jit_view.rs), which the live chunk and the view implement, so the two keep one code.
(b) One compile thread a process (opt::compiler, `trust-js-opt`, started at the first job, serving
every engine); a checkpoint queues the job, its asking chunk marked compiling with no checkpoint
armed; the compile thread sets the poll word's install bit (POLL_INSTALL, release) and the next
poll of generated code (jit_interrupt) installs the result after the checks no run-time guard
covers (the running realm, a discard since the snapshot, the function collected, the chunk
superseded, a recompile published meanwhile), with an instruction barrier, or drops it and arms
the checkpoint or warm-up again; the second stage's publication and the chain recompile's and the
reoptimization's installs moved to the install (Interp::publish_recompile, opt::Landing);
synchronous with one CPU (available_parallelism, the affinity mask), under TRUST_JS_OPT_SYNC=1,
where the thread cannot start, and in tests pinned so; an engine dropped with jobs pending cancels
them; the log's `[opt] main-thread recompile|snapshot|stubs|install|fallback` lines (their timers
leaving out the log's own printing), `on the main|compile thread, queued <ms> ms, installed <ms> ms
after its snapshot`, `[opt] dropped`, and a rerun's `<n> runs, <ms> ms before the last`. The
review's finding (5b16206e): the property templates read a site's ways through
JitChunk::jit_cache_ways (the view's copy), not the live cache, and the live chunk's JitChunk
methods that read mutable state assert in debug builds that no compile off the main thread calls
them. After the first measurement (0a580e91): the stack effects and the capture TDZ facts made on
the compile thread from the operations (bytecode::stack_effect, a move checked line for line), only
the constants an Op::Const pushes copied (debug builds assert no other is read), the keys looked up
only for compiles off the main thread, the job and the mapped code's words freed on the compile
thread.
- Measured (fat LTO, 8d4cd523's code against main da6933eb; the fixtures on A725 CPUs 10-11,
  setarch -R, medians of 5; Octane's fixed runs on CPUs 10-13, medians of 3). The bar's sum (the
  main-thread parts, the fallbacks beside), main to branch: Djot 21.79 to 4.52 ms (recompile 2.82,
  snapshot 1.27, stubs 0.18, install 0.26), React 3.06 to 0.73, Vue 8.64 to 3.04; as shares of the
  log runs' timed loops 2.88%, 1.46% and 0.82% (main 12.9%, 6.0%, 2.29%), with the fallbacks 4.15%,
  2.10% and 0.89%: under 3% on A725 cores; the X925 two-CPU session is the bar's reading. The main
  thread's instructions in the measured pass -8.20%, -0.70% and -0.09% (cycles -7.55%, -2.20%,
  -3.00%), the process's +2.97%, +1.20% and +0.05%; passes 2-10's main thread -1.07%, -0.05%,
  -0.02%. Against the design's expectation (offthread-input.txt 3.3): generate's main-thread part
  is 9.0%, 13.3% and 18.1% of main's generate where it expected 4.9%, 5.8% and 7.8%, the excess all
  the snapshot line's (6.7-10.5% of generate against the feedback snapshot's 2.6%: it also holds
  the freeze's copies and the facts it forces, and off the main thread the key lookups and the
  hand-off, about 11 us a job); the install 1.4-2.7% (the design's 2.2%); template code running
  longer while compiles are pending about 0.5% of Djot's pass (an estimate); snapshot to install
  0.78 ms mean and 5.2 ms most on Djot (a compile that ran twice). Octane: the main thread -0.66%,
  the process +0.14% (Richards -8.94% and +11.60%: three more compiles, callees that main's tier
  code splices reaching their own checkpoints while called from template code, and two reruns),
  the bar's sum 935 to 167 ms, the synchronous mode +0.05%. Reruns for missing stubs or atoms:
  Djot 3, React 3, Vue 2, Octane 44 of 1,302 compiles (30.5 ms of the compile thread, 2.9 ms of the
  main thread). The tier off: Octane +0.07% (Earley-Boyer +0.94%, PdfJS +0.33%, TypeScript +0.27%),
  all of it the install bit's three-line check in jit_interrupt (a build without just those lines
  counts as main): the collector scans the native stack conservatively under jit_interrupt's
  frame, and the check's code changes which stale words it finds (Earley-Boyer's first minor
  collection keeps 5,367 objects against 5,328; 75 collections against 74): stack-scan luck, not
  work. Reported to the owner as small.
- Tests: part (a)'s masked code hashes identical to main on the five workloads and in the tier's
  tests (481); (b)'s synchronous code differs from main's by Interp's offsets alone (+40 bytes, the
  pending compiles' list); the off-thread tests (8: the install at the poll; drops after a
  discard's flags, a publication meanwhile, a superseded chunk and a collected function, each with
  the tier-up after; an engine dropped with jobs pending; forced exits; one CPU); 9 mutation checks,
  all caught. The library's suites with the tier off, on eager synchronous and on eager
  asynchronous (3,149 each with the embed feature; the asynchronous twice, one beside a
  synchronous run for load), the tier's tests with exits every 7 and with an exit limit of 1 (251
  each), the poll and Agent tests in both modes; Test262 in full, 53,573 of 53,575 in each of the
  four configurations (off, on, eager, exits every 100) on main and the branch, the same two
  staging/sm failures; clippy as main, fmt clean; zombie (x86-64): the check with tests, clippy
  with -D warnings, Windows' check (date.rs's 20 warnings), the library's suite (2,773). wasm32
  unchecked (no target installed on either machine).
- Departures: (1) the stubs and atoms a job finds missing are made on the main thread at the
  install's poll, which runs the job again (Outcome::Needs), rather than all made before the first
  job, which would assemble stubs no workload calls and atoms the template JIT never makes; (2) the
  process's OnceLock switches the shared templates read are primed on the main thread
  (jit::prime_switches); (3) Interp's 40 bytes move the offsets generated code embeds, so (b)'s
  identity is the code dump's; (4) five library tests that read a compile's results at once
  compile on the main thread (opt::pin_synchronous_for_test, point 8), and the inline-context
  tests wait for pending compiles (opt::finish_compiles_for_test, as V8's %FinalizeOptimization);
  the off-thread tests return on one CPU; (5) the main-thread lines leave out the log's printing
  (eprintln! writes each formatted piece to unbuffered stderr, tens of us a compiled line, most of
  the first round's install line).
- Open: the snapshot line's copies (the chunk's immutable operations and names shared with the
  compile thread behind an Arc, and its remaining pure facts made there, would cut it); the
  reruns' latency; tests that read the tier's state right after an evaluation may still fail
  rarely in the asynchronous configuration (finish_compiles_for_test is the fix's form).

The fixtures' timer landed (b7b2a1cd..3cd1b05f, 2026-11-10; target/p3/stage2/perfnow-impl), as designed:
Engine::unstable_install_shell_performance (builtins/host.rs install_shell_performance: a plain
non-enumerable performance object, now() of length 0 reading a process-wide OnceLock<Instant>
origin set at the first install), the shell's call after Engine::new() and its --help's Globals;
the fixtures' timer lines (new hashes in PROVENANCE.txt; the lines before the first timer line
byte-identical, the warm copies checked equal up to it); fx3 under target/p3/stage2. Tests:
crates/trust-js/tests/shell_performance.rs (the object's shape; under each tier, 10,000 readings
never decreasing with a step under a millisecond, the first under a minute, a 20 ms busy wait by
Date.now() reading 19 ms or more and under a second), each assertion's mutation caught through a
prelude on the built shell (seconds, microseconds, whole milliseconds, the wall clock, a step
backwards; enumerable, a length of 1, absent); clippy -D warnings on the crate, fmt. The fixtures'
outputs carry PROVENANCE's fields on the shell and on Node.
A finding while making fx3: fx2's Vue passes 2-10 ran after the fixture's reaction.stop(), so they
timed the reactive model's writes with the effect unsubscribed (invocations stay at 12,359; Node
about 64 ms a pass against 86 ms with the effect), not the measured pass's work. Stage 1's and
stage 2's steady-state Vue rows (fx2) measured that lighter work, the tier on and off alike: their
on/off comparisons hold for it, not for Vue's steady state as its measured pass defines the work.
fx3 runs the nine passes before reaction.stop() and checks the work (invocations 113,609 after
them, on Node and on the shell). fx2's Djot printed the time since its start (all ten sets) where
the others print the measured pass; no report read that field, and fx3 prints the measured pass.

The interim two-CPU session (2026-11-10; target/p3/stage2/session-2cpu-p3s2-4509bd62, report.txt;
main 4509bd62: step 12 and the shell's performance.now()). The bar's phases only (the fixtures,
their steady state on fx3, the calls, Octane, the tier's log); ARES-6 and Web Tooling wait for the
exit session. Every engine and reference run on CPUs 5 and 6, both uncapped through every phase
(0 capped seconds); the owner's own programs busy 2.5-3 cores elsewhere meanwhile
(load-2cpu-p3s2-4509bd62.log).
- The 2% bar, the tier on against off: Vue's measured pass -11.87% (177.31 against 201.2 ms),
  Djot's -8.70% (70.21 against 76.9; +4.11% in p3s2-7f92ab9d), React's +2.96% (30.99 against
  30.1; +6.67% before); the steady states (fx3) Vue -11.98%, React -2.39%, Djot -13.15%; the calls
  within 2% (direct-call +1.66% the largest); Octane every benchmark faster or equal but CodeLoad,
  +10.66% (12,887 against 14,261); the geomean +27.25%.
- The compile bar (the main-thread parts; the fallbacks beside): Vue 1.07% (1.89 ms: recompile
  1.16, snapshot 0.42, stubs 0.17, install 0.10), React 1.51% (0.47 ms), Djot 4.54% (3.19 ms:
  recompile 2.03, snapshot 0.87, stubs 0.11, install 0.14). Djot misses: step 14 removes its empty
  plans' recompiles (the design: 0.69 ms), step 15 its splices at sites with several callees or
  closures, and the snapshot line's copies are step 12's open item.
- Against node-mg on the same two CPUs (the P3 exit's yardstick, decision 1: within 2x): the
  measured passes Vue 3.82x, React 3.10x, Djot 2.86x; the steady states 3.94x, 3.42x, 4.13x;
  Octane's geomean over the 15 benchmarks Node runs 4.13x (the tier off 5.41x).
- CodeLoad (target/p3/stage2/codeload-diag): the compile thread makes the process multi-threaded,
  and glibc's malloc then leaves its single-thread fast path (arena locks and atomics on each call
  its 7-entry tcache does not serve). The shell's allocator (fastalloc::ClassAlloc: a per-thread
  cache of blocks up to 1 KB, 256 KB a class, each miss and overflow one glibc call) sends
  CodeLoad's parse bursts there: 210,842 allocations and 209,931 frees in its 5 fixed runs, 96% of
  1 KB or less. An idle second thread (an LD_PRELOAD shim) costs the tier-off engine +10.1%
  cycles and +1.25% instructions on CodeLoad's fixed runs (7 rounds), the session's gap; glibc's
  malloc and free go from about 11% of its cycles to 22.6% (perf: __libc_malloc2, the locking
  path, 7.4%). With glibc's tcache deepened (GLIBC_TUNABLES tcache_count=65535) the run is -3.3%
  single-threaded and +1.2% with the idle thread. The tier-on runs without a compile (5 fixed
  runs reach none) equal the tier off. The fixtures' measured passes call glibc 700 to 6,900
  times and lose nothing measurable to an idle thread (cycles React -0.93%, Djot -3.91%, Vue
  -0.01%). The exemplars' parsers allocate their trees in arenas, never per node through malloc:
  V8's Zone (src/zone/zone.h:43, the bump allocation at 55-67; AstNodeFactory, src/ast/ast.h:3069,
  zone_->New at 3082), SpiderMonkey's LifoAlloc (js/src/ds/LifoAlloc.h:690; allocParseNode,
  frontend/FullParseHandler.h:49, through ParseNodeAllocator, frontend/ParseNode.h:2649), JSC's
  ParserArena (parser/ParserArena.h:165 allocateFreeable; ParserArenaFreeable's operator new,
  parser/Nodes.h:110-123); and their allocators refill per-thread caches in batches from slabs.
  For the owner: the fix's direction (below, in the report of 2026-11-10).
- React at equal work (codeload-diag/fixtures-summary.txt; user cycles on CPUs 5-6 under the
  1.378 GHz cap, GC pacing off): the tier on synchronous +5.8% cycles at +0.13% instructions,
  asynchronous +7.7% (the process, the compile thread included) at +1.21%: the deficit is the tier
  code's cycles at equal work, not compile work; step 15 removes its WrongCallee exits and their
  discard and recompile.

Step 13, measured (2026-11-10; target/p3/stage2/step13-impl, report.txt and its "OPTION (c)";
branches p3-step13 31abeba0 and p3-step13c 7558959b, neither landed). As designed (a), the rule
holds: a8 and fn(b,d,f,g) are discarded at their loop limit after 5 exits in place of 100, a8's
second-stage share of a zlib run falls from 26.3% to 2.2%, zlib's coverage 69.0% -> 96.9%, its
fixed runs -40.7% instructions. But the VM warm-up costs where exited frames past the loop limit
loop fewer than 128 times: those loops now run in the VM, TRust's slowest tier, where JSC's run
in baseline code: DeltaBlue +39.4%, Box2D +11.5%, Crypto +2.1%, React's full run +10.2%, Djot's
measured pass +1.7% (Octane's geomean -0.89%). And most of zlib's gain is not the rule's: the
VM's iterations fill the value and element records that template code's in-line hits never
record (rule U, feedback-schema.txt 5.1 and 5.2), so zlib's four recompiles come out 13-25%
smaller in bytes; without the warm-up (hotv: the first transfer past the loop limit discards) the
recompiles are main's to the byte and zlib gains -14.4% (geomean -1.08%; Crypto +2.6%, Djot +1.3%
and +0.85%). JSC's mechanism, read again: every exit that does not jettison resets the baseline
counter to the long warm-up (dfg/DFGOSRExitCompilerCommon.cpp:105-141; 1,000, the warm-up a
jettison's alternative waits too, runtime/OptionsList.h:373-374, bytecode/CodeBlock.cpp:2412-2414,
2641-2669), and while an optimized replacement exists only baseline loops reach that counter,
since calls are relinked to the replacement (jit/JITOperations.cpp:2947-2966). Option (c) does
that with the template JIT as the baseline: each exit at or past the loop limit that does not
discard arms (and re-arms) a per-chunk loop word at warm_up_gap(R, 0); template code's loop
iterations (step 8's counting, step 11's weights) count toward it and entries never do (its own
word, Chunk::loop_retry_at; the entry word stays idle while it is armed); the crossing copies it
into the entry word, and the next entry's try_inline_recompile takes the loop limit's check and
discards. Exited frames transfer at their first back edge as on main. Measured: Octane's geomean
-1.21% (the best of the four), zlib -14.25% (a8 at 5; the other three stay under the warm-up
between exits), GBEMU -5.5%, DeltaBlue +0.05%, Crypto +0.56%, Box2D +1.55% (three of its
minified functions now discarded at their loop limit and reoptimized; not root-caused), React 0,
Djot's measured pass +0.02% and passes 2-10 +0.25% (the due path's store; a cheaper form clears
the loop word at the crossing, so that a due chunk's loops count nothing at 3 instructions). The
tier off +0.02%. For the owner (asked 2026-11-11): (c) recommended, with the cheaper due path,
rebased onto step 14; rule U's record filling to be designed as its own item after step 15 (JSC's
baseline ORs a double result into the arith profile on its in-line path,
jit/JITAddGenerator.cpp:158-160, bytecode/ArithProfile.cpp:82-93; V8's Sparkplug calls typed
stubs chosen by the feedback, baseline/baseline-compiler.cc:1269-1317; SpiderMonkey's baseline
ICs attach a stub per type, so a new type misses and is recorded).

Step 14 landed (8d1803f1..66b90635, 2026-11-11; target/p3/stage2/step14-impl). As designed, with
the tier on: a function whose inline plan splices nothing gets no second chunk; the tier compiles
its first stage in place (opt::compile_in_place; off the main thread Landing::InPlace, dropped at
the install by step 12's checks and its own: the function collected, the first stage superseded,
a second stage published, a discard that flagged its sites since the snapshot), and the install
puts the tier's code in the chunk's own slot, keeps the template code as the fallback
(ChunkProfile::kept, with its own CLOCK registration; CLOCK may evict either code, and every
eviction moves the epoch), and moves the chunk's entry word, not the epoch. A discard returns to
the kept code, compiling only where CLOCK evicted it. Departures: (1) the entry word is a byte in
Chunk's last padding byte, not a u32 (any u32 field moves inline_attempted, which the template
code embeds, so the tier off would not stay byte-identical); between two epoch moves it changes
at most twice (an install in place, a supersession), so a way whose epoch matches never sees it
wrap; CallIc::entry is the u32 at 60. (2) The kept code lives on the chunk's tier record, for the
same reason; where the code cache admits no slot for it, the template code is retired and the
epoch moves. (3) The Rust probes (jit_call_hit, resolve_cached_call's ways, overflow table and
code-keyed retry, prepared_call_current, construct_jit_fast, call_compiled_target) compare the word
with the tier on and off, since testing the switch costs what the compare does; supersede moves
the word too (66b90635), so the word answers alone, 2 instructions a probe more than main's
superseded test. (4) A reoptimization's install keeps the replaced template code for every chunk.
(5) The direct sequence's compare is 3 instructions (the way's copy rides the LDP that loads the
callee chunk; LDRB, CMP, B.NE). (6) main's a_construct_site_picks_up_its_constructors_new_code is
pinned synchronous (065b4116): flaky with the off-thread compile (about 1 run in 20). Tests:
opt::inplace_tests (13: the first stage carrying the tier's code with no second chunk; a way
filled before the install entering the tier's code with no epoch move through the direct sequence,
jit_call_hit, resolve_cached_call, prepared_call_current, a construct site and the overflow table;
the discard's return and its compile when the kept code was evicted; the warm-up and the
reoptimization; the done test; the chain recompile superseding a first stage that carries the
tier's code; a superseded job dropped; the exit profile read by a caller's splice) and
jit::entry_word_tests; 13 mutations caught; the library's suites in every configuration (3,163),
the tier's (265, three configurations), poll and Agent (51, both modes); Test262 53,573 of 53,575
in four configurations; zombie's check, clippy -D warnings, Windows' check and suite (2,773); the
tier off's template code byte-identical on 21 workloads (PdfJS and TypeScript made deterministic).
Measured (fat LTO, A725 CPUs 12-13, instructions, against main 4509bd62):
- The empty plans' second stages are gone: the fixtures' 56 a run (Vue 29, React 6, Djot 21),
  Octane's 611 of 1,010 (60.9% of the census; the plan's 60.3%), each an install in place; tier
  compiles unchanged (1,300); Octane's fallback compiles 309 -> 139 (167 discards return to the
  kept code); peak RSS Djot -100 KiB, the others unchanged.
- The main thread's recompile line a run: Djot 3.00 -> 2.20 ms, React 0.38 -> 0.22, Vue 1.86 ->
  1.28; the bar's sum on A725 log runs Djot 2.98% -> 2.45% of its pass (the plan: about 2.6%),
  React 1.51% -> 1.27%, Vue 0.89% -> 0.73%. The two-CPU session reads the bar.
- The compare's cost, which the plan named but did not estimate: +3.00 instructions a direct call
  with the tier on (none off), +2.00 a Rust probe on and off. With the tier on, the fixtures'
  measured passes Vue +0.25%, React +0.46%, Djot +0.03%, passes 2-10 +0.25%, +0.52% and +0.08%;
  Octane's geomean -0.008% asynchronous and -0.184% synchronous (Box2D -2.6%: 108 recompiles and 6
  fallback compiles fewer; Earley-Boyer +0.6% to +0.9%); the tier off +0.029% (RayTrace +0.30%,
  its constructions' probe).
So on call-heavy code the compare costs more than the recompiles it removes, and the exemplars
pay no per-call compare: JSC relinks a code block's incoming calls when code changes
(bytecode/CodeBlock.cpp:2221-2228, linkIncomingCall and unlinkOrUpgradeIncomingCalls), V8's calls
load the entry through the callee's dispatch handle (codegen/arm64/macro-assembler-arm64.cc:4141,
LoadEntrypointFromJSDispatchTable), and SpiderMonkey's load the callee script's jitCodeRaw (its
header word, vm/JSScript.h:1761). Which of these TRust takes is a design item after step 15, with
the probes' floor of one instruction.

The owner's answers (2026-11-11): "JSC's form for step 13 is approved, as is your recommendation
for refilling each thread from its own pages." Step 13 lands as option (c) with the cheaper due
path, rebased onto step 14 (its agent builds that version now). The shell allocator's fix is
designed below.

The shell allocator's spans (design, 2026-11-11; input: the interim session's CodeLoad finding,
target/p3/stage2/codeload-diag). fastalloc::ClassAlloc, the engine shell's global allocator (the
library registers none; the test262 runner and the difftest keep their own, and the runtime's CLI
uses the system's), keeps a per-thread intrusive free list for each 16-byte class up to 1 KiB,
capped at 256 KiB a class, and takes every miss from the system and gives every overflow back to
it, one call a block. Once the compile thread exists, glibc takes its locking path on each of
those calls: CodeLoad's parse bursts make about 210,000 such allocations and 210,000 such frees a
5-run fixed set, and an idle second thread alone costs the tier-off engine +10.1% cycles there.
JSC's libpas never takes a lock per small block: a thread's local allocator for a size class
allocates from a page it holds, by bump or from a copy of the page's free bits
(pas_local_allocator.h:60-80), and takes another page from the size class's directory when that
one is spent (pas_local_allocator_refill_with_known_config, pas_local_allocator_inlines.h:444);
a thread's frees go to a thread-local deallocation log (pas_thread_local_cache.h:57-63, appended
at 389-403; 100 entries, pas_internal_config.h:91), flushed to their pages in batches; a scavenger
decommits empty pages every 100 ms (pas_scavenger.c:70; pas_segregated_page_take_empty_granules,
pas_segregated_page.c:316). The design takes that shape:
1. Unchanged: the per-thread lists and their pop and push (the fast paths), the 16-byte classes up
   to 1 KiB, the 256 KiB cap a class, large and over-aligned blocks from the system, realloc
   within a class for free, alloc_zeroed clearing class blocks, the exit drain, trim after a
   major collection (gc_collect's reclaimed >= 50,000).
2. Spans: class blocks come from 64 KiB spans at 64 KiB boundaries, each of one class, carved from
   2 MiB segments mapped from the OS (the collector's os_pages: mmap, VirtualAlloc on Windows),
   never from the system allocator. A span's header, in its first 64 bytes, holds its class, the
   list of its blocks returned to it, its bump cursor over blocks never handed out, the count of
   its blocks out (in use or in a thread's list) and its links in its class's list of spans with
   free blocks.
3. A thread's miss: its list empty, it takes blocks from its own bump region of the class (a span
   tail it holds, as libpas's local allocator holds a page's bump region), carving one block a
   miss with nothing to lock; with no region left it refills under the class's lock: from the
   class's first span with free blocks, that span's whole returned list into the thread's list and
   its bump tail as the thread's region, else a span from the empty pool or a new segment. One
   lock round trip per span's worth of blocks.
4. A thread's overflow: a free that finds the thread's list at its cap returns a batch, the colder
   part of the list (bounded in bytes, a quarter to a half of the cap; the step says which and
   why), to their spans under the class's lock, each onto its span's returned list (the span
   found by masking the address), its out count down; a span with blocks again joins its class's
   list; a span whose count reaches zero leaves it for the empty pool. One lock round trip per
   batch, as libpas's log flush.
5. Memory back to the OS: empty spans wait in a pool for any class; trim, after a major
   collection, returns the thread's lists to their spans and discards the empty pool's memory
   (madvise MADV_DONTNEED on Linux, keeping the mapping: it reads as zero when reused; the other
   systems' equivalents where the collector has them), and the pool beyond a committed bound is
   discarded at once. Where libpas's scavenger decommits on a timer, the engine has a natural
   point already, its major collections, and no thread of its own for a timer.
6. Threads: a block freed on another thread joins that thread's list, as today, and goes back to
   its span at that thread's overflow, trim or exit; spans are global, under their class's lock,
   so ownership needs nothing more. A thread's exit returns its lists and its bump regions to
   their spans; a free after that goes to its span at once.
7. The allocator never allocates (its lists and spans are intrusive, its locks std's const-built
   mutexes, its memory mapped directly), so no path re-enters it.
Expected: no class block reaches glibc, so the compile thread's existence no longer changes their
cost (CodeLoad's +10.66% with the tier on; about 4% of its blocks are over 1 KiB and still go to
the system), and the burst pattern costs a lock round trip per batch where it cost a malloc or
free per block (deepening glibc's tcache, the nearest stand-in, gave CodeLoad -3.3% single-
threaded). Risks: class-segregated spans cannot lend memory across classes as malloc's heap
does, so a span pinned by a few live blocks holds its memory (peak and post-trim RSS measured
against main on the fixtures and Octane, Splay, TypeScript, PdfJS, CodeLoad and zlib among them,
with fresh copies of both binaries and an empty-script control); a burst at a class's lock while
another thread holds it (the compile thread's own classes rarely meet the main thread's at the
same moment). Tests: the allocator's own (the classes' blocks from spans, the bump region, the
refill and overflow batches, the returned lists, an emptied span to the pool and its reuse by
another class, trim's discard and the memory reading zero after it, cross-thread frees, a
thread's exit, frees after exit, alloc_zeroed, realloc across classes, a randomized
multi-threaded stress with contents checked); the shell's tests; the x86-64 and Windows checks.
Measured: CodeLoad's fixed runs (tier off and on, alone and with the idle-thread shim, cycles
and instructions), the calls that still reach glibc (codeload-diag's malloc-hist.so), Octane's
fixed runs, the fixtures' measured passes and passes 2-10, peak and post-trim RSS; the two-CPU
session reads the bar.

Step 15 landed (6d328c5d..47a6197a, 2026-11-11; target/p3/stage2/step15-impl). As designed: with
the tier on, plan_inlines_at splices no call site whose ways hold more than one distinct callee
and none that met another closure of a way's function, at every depth, before any way is charged
to the budget (bytecode::splice_rule_declines; TRUST_JS_TIER_LOG names each skip); the tier off
keeps P2's plans. The record adds no instruction to any hit: a site's code hint starts at 4, the
way past the last, whose hinted-way test reads the site's own guard word (CallSite::hint_guard, an
odd byte 4 x 112 + 48 bytes past the first way, inside the 504-byte site, whose offsets and size
are unchanged; every function identity is a multiple of 8, so the test's first compare fails and
no read leaves the site; compile-asserted, and a test runs it against an unreadable page after the
site), so a site's first code-keyed hit takes the full probe, which writes the hint as before (108
instructions in place of 35, once a site). The one-instruction fallback was not needed. The Rust
paths record too (CallSite::note_code_hit: resolve_cached_call_entry's code-sharing retry, every
call's on x86-64, and jit_call_hit's live closure), and a recompile's site starts with its first
stage's hint. Departures: (1) point 5: the reoptimization does not plan again (it recompiles the
same chunk), and the discard's WrongCallee flag already makes it take the guard's mismatch edge,
so nothing was added (a test covers it); the late closures are recorded on the second stage's own
site, which plans from the first stage do not read. (2) The x86-64 JIT has no hinted test or probe
in machine code; its calls take the generic helper, whose Rust retry records. (3) jit_call_hit's
record is a backstop no mutation can catch: the hit that reaches it was recorded by the machine
code already. (4) The tier off is not instruction-identical: a site's first code-keyed hit takes
the full probe, a closure from the overflow table takes the generic helper until a full-probe hit
writes the hint, and the Rust paths load and compare a byte; within 0.08% everywhere (Octane's
geomean +0.00%, calls.js within 0.0002%); its template code is main's up to heap addresses (40 of
3,067 compiles hash differently, a heap address taking one MOVZ/MOVK fewer; normalized, 2,423 of
2,423 alike on the deterministic workloads). (5) callee_pickup_tests' rust_callers_pick_up_new_code
is pinned synchronous (47a6197a): an asynchronous flake under load. Tests: opt::splice_rule_tests,
tier_tests' late second closure, jit::call_record_tests (the guard page among them),
call_feedback_tests' record on both architectures, callee_pickup_tests' polymorphic site adapted;
6 of 7 mutations caught (the seventh is (3)); the library's suites (3,174 in every configuration),
the tier's (273, three configurations), poll and Agent (51, both modes); Test262 off and eager
53,573 of 53,575 (main's two failures); zombie's check, clippy -D warnings, Windows' check and
suite (2,774); wasm32 unchecked.
Measured (fat LTO, A725 CPUs 10-11, instructions, against main 66b90635; the tier on synchronous,
the asynchronous mode alike):
- The fixtures: React's measured pass -3.36% (the plan: -3.4%), 2.82% under the tier off; Djot's
  -0.99% and its passes 2-10 -1.76% (-0.9%, -1.7%); Vue +0.02% and -0.09% (unchanged). Every
  WrongCallee exit on the fixtures is gone (React 100 -> 0, Djot 200 -> 0). Djot's pass stays
  +4.77% over the tier off (the plan: +4.6%).
- Octane: -1.78% geomean (-1.97% asynchronous; the plan: -1.8%): Richards -12.54%, DeltaBlue
  -7.56%, RayTrace -3.91% (-12.5%, -7.7%, -3.9%); Splay, Mandreel, Crypto, zlib and TypeScript
  unchanged. WrongCallee exits 24,816 -> 7,653, discards 305 -> 253, installs in place 610 -> 637
  (plans the rule empties take step 14's path). The tier on against off: -11.27% (main -9.66%).
- The compile bar is not moved, against the interim session's expectation. Djot's main-thread
  sum in single asynchronous log runs (indicative) goes 3.96 -> 4.39 ms: recompile 2.15 -> 2.17,
  snapshot 1.34 -> 1.63, stubs 0.19 -> 0.23, install 0.28 -> 0.36. It compiles 7 functions more
  (callees no longer spliced, which main's run never compiles), and these cost what the removed
  discards' reoptimizations save; its fallback compiles 2.12 -> 1.50 ms. React 0.67 -> 0.48 ms.
  What is left of Djot's bar is the recompile and the snapshot's copies (step 12's open item).
- The rule's cost: where closures of one function share a site and one of them is hot
  (step15-impl/tools/closures.js, not one of the bar's call microbenchmarks), the tier on is
  +4.96% against main and +4.75% over the tier off (main -0.20%). Main splices way 0's closure;
  the rule splices none of them until stage 3 guards by code.

Djot's compile bar after steps 14 and 15 (study, 2026-11-11; target/p3/stage2/
compile-bar-input.txt, evidence compile-bar/). On main 3a79e650 Djot's main-thread parts a run
on an A725 core (medians of 7 log runs): recompile 2.07 ms (15 recompiles), snapshot about 1.5 ms
(47 jobs; 0.93 synchronous), stubs 0.21 (3 reruns), install 0.28. By the interim session's
per-part ratios that is about 2.80 ms on X925, 4.0% (3.9-4.3%) of Djot's pass, against 3%
(2.09 ms). React is about 1.2% and Vue 0.96%. Where it goes:
- The recompile, 53%: the AST body walk 63% of it, finish_chunk 17.5%, the seeds 7.8%. Its cost
  follows the recompiled caller's size (0.6 us an output operation): next() (512 operations)
  costs 0.29 ms to splice 75 operations of callees, feed() (491) 0.24 ms to splice 12, both
  through the small-callee budget alone.
- The snapshot, synchronously: Snapshot::take_in 61% (a 168-byte SiteView for every pc, 1.08 MB a
  run, where 1,175 of 5,473 pcs carry feedback) and Chunk::freeze 33% (340 KB copied a run, 46%
  of it the named sites' ways, which take_in has just read; the operations 39%). Asynchronously
  also the names' key lookups (381 a run, 2.7 us a job, all found) and the hand-off (the queue,
  and a futex wake at every enqueue: 5-10 us a job on two cores, by difference).
- The install line holds the log's own formatting: about 3.2 us an install, half the line.
- Step 15's 7 extra compiles pay back: refusing them costs Djot's measured pass +0.87%
  instructions and passes 2-10 +1.22% cycles. They stay.
- Outside the bar's parts: the settled-feedback fingerprint at the checkpoints costs about as much
  main-thread time as the snapshot line (0.97M cycles a run).
The exemplars copy no bytecode on the main thread. V8's Maglev reads the BytecodeArray and its
constant pool through the handle on the background thread (src/compiler/heap-refs.h:1150-1152),
and the feedback through the broker as it builds (src/compiler/js-heap-broker.cc:926-934).
SpiderMonkey's main-thread snapshot walks the ops but copies only the Baseline ICs' stub data,
sparsely (jit/WarpOracle.cpp:310-336 and 1333-1352; jit/WarpSnapshot.h:53-56), into one LifoAlloc
reused across compiles (jit/Ion.cpp:1795-1845); WarpBuilder reads the bytecode and its atoms off
thread. JSC's DFG parses the baseline's shared instructions on its own thread and reads the
profiles under the block's lock (dfg/DFGByteCodeParser.cpp:8338, 10582). All three carry property
names as atoms in the bytecode (V8's constant pool, interpreter/bytecode-array-builder.cc:942-947;
SpiderMonkey's gcthings, vm/JSScript.h:2361-2377; JSC's identifiers, bytecode/
UnlinkedCodeBlock.h:196). None wakes a busy compile thread: SpiderMonkey's dispatch returns while
every helper runs a task (vm/HelperThreads.cpp:262-265), JSC wakes threads only below a load-based
target (jit/JITWorklist.cpp:106-148), and V8 posts a worker only below its two-thread cap
(libplatform/default-job.cc:42-62). JSC's compiler threads make a missing shared thunk themselves,
under the thunks' lock (jit/JITThunks.cpp:189-222).
Estimated savings on X925:
- the exemplars' snapshot, keys, hand-off and stubs, and the instrument's fix: about 0.63 ms
  (0.5-0.75), to about 3.1% (2.9-3.5%), not reliably under 3%;
- with an interim splice rule (no recompile of a caller over the shared budget for small-callee
  splices alone): about 2.5%;
- stage 3's graph inlining, which recompiles no bytecode: about 1.7% with the stubs and
  instrument items.
The interim rule is TRust's trade, not an exemplar's (Maglev inlines small callees whatever the
caller's size, as it pays no recompile); its Octane measurement is under way, and the choice
between it, stage 3's inlining and carrying the bar goes to the owner with that and the next
session's readings.

Step 16, the snapshot and the hand-off in the exemplars' shape (design, 2026-11-11; input
compile-bar-input.txt sections 2, 4 and 5). Each part is needed whichever way the bar is met: with
an interim splice rule, for the margin; with stage 3, because inlined callees add snapshots and
jobs.
1. The instrument. The main-thread timers stop before the log's formatting: log_main_thread reads
   the clock before it formats the chunk's name, and the install's timer ends before finish
   formats its compiled line. The bar's parts then time the engine's work. Nothing changes without
   the log.
2. Keys carried by the chunk. The frozen view takes a named operation's key from its chunk's key
   table (Chunk::name_keys), made once a chunk, instead of looking up its text each job. A first
   stage's table is the one the VM made, or is made at its first freeze, on the main thread, as
   the VM's first named access makes it. A second stage's table comes from its first stage's and
   its spliced callees' tables at the recompile; the names the recompile adds are made there.
   This is the exemplars' atoms in the bytecode. Constraints: the tier off pays nothing it does
   not pay now, and no job reruns for a missing atom.
3. The hand-off. The compile thread marks itself waiting under the queue's lock before it waits,
   and enqueue notifies only a waiting thread, which is SpiderMonkey's and JSC's rule. The job's
   clock reads (its Timing fields, the queue's stamp) happen only under the log.
4. The snapshot sparse, and decoded on the compile thread, as SpiderMonkey's oracle copies the IC
   data that cannot be read off thread and WarpBuilder interprets it there.
   - On the main thread: one raw copy of each feedback-bearing site's words (a named site's
     preferred way and ways, which the property templates' view then reads in place of the
     freeze's second read; a call site's ways; the value, element and construct records through
     the record map), the free names' global views (they read the realm) and the exit profile.
     This replaces a SiteView for every pc.
   - On the compile thread: the SiteViews, decoded from the raw copies by the same decode, made
     generic over its source as step 12 made the emitters (jit::JitChunk).
   The step settles the raw copy's form, and says what it holds and why the decode cannot see a
   difference.
5. The job's allocations in one arena that the next job reuses (SpiderMonkey's LifoAlloc reuse,
